UPDATED VERSION 
POLLUTED RIVER 
STRETCHES FOR 
RESTORATION OF 
WATER QUALITY  
 
 
PREFACE 
 
 
Water quality data of water bodies in the country forms the basis of management and 
Planning of water pollution control. CPCB has been assessing water quality of aquatic 
resources in the country in association with State Pollution Control Boards and Pollution 
Control Committees for Union Territories under National Water Quality Monitoring Network 
(NWMP). At present, the network consists of 4736 locations spread in 28 States and 7 Union 
Territories across the country. Based on periodic assessment of water quality of rivers in the 
country, CPCB identifies polluted river stretches (PRS) considering Bio-Chemical Oxygen 
Demand (BOD) as a criteria parameter. The analysis helps in preparation and 
implementation of appropriate action plans for restoration of identified polluted river 
stretches. The present assessment of ‘Polluted River Stretches for Restoration of Water 
Quality – 2025’ is based on the aforementioned criteria used for periodic analysis of water 
quality data in the past. Of the 623 rivers assessed, there are 296 polluted stretches 
identified in 271 rivers in 32 States & Union Territories. The present assessment of polluted 
river stretches indicates that the number of polluted river stretches has decreased from 351 
in 2018 assessment to 279 in 2025 assessment. Further, marked improvement in previously 
identified 219 PRS has been observed, and 148 PRS no longer remain in the list of PRS. 
 
I am hopeful that this study will be of immense use to all concerned stakeholders and 
agencies in taking corrective measures to achieve the objective of restoration of identified 
Polluted River Stretches. 
 
 
(Vir Vikram Yadav)  
Chairman

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) executes National Water Quality Monitoring 
Programme (NWMP) in association with State Pollution Control Boards/ Pollution Control 
Committees for assessing the water quality of aquatic resources in the country. Presently 
the monitoring under NWMP is carried out at 4736 locations in the country including 2155 
locations on rivers. The present study is in series of periodic assessment of water quality of 
rivers in the country conducted by CPCB in terms of exceedances observed with respect to 
criteria parameter, Bio-Chemical Oxygen Demand (BOD) for identification of polluted river 
stretches/ locations where water quality is required to be restored with interventions and 
time targeted action plans are required.  
 
CPCB initiated the exercise of identifying polluted river stretches (PRS) in the country since 
2009 based on the river water quality monitored during the years 2002 - 2008 in 29 States/ 
UTs, and afterwards reports were brought out in 2015 based on assessment of water quality 
data monitored during the years 2009-2012 in 27 States/ UTs, in 2018 based on water 
quality data of the years 2016 & 2017 in 31 States/ UTs. Total 150 PRS on 121 rivers, 302 
PRS on 275 rivers, and 351 PRS on 323 rivers were identified in year 2009, 2015 and 2018. 
In the year 2015, 70% of rivers monitored (275 out of 390) were identified as polluted, 
whereas, in the year 2022, only 46% of rivers monitored (279 out of 603) were identified as 
polluted. 
 
The present assessment is based on the river water quality data of the years 2022 & 2023. 
Out of 2116 locations on rivers monitored during 2022 & 2023, 1312 locations (62%) were 
complying with BOD criteria of less than 3.0 mg/L, notified under Primary Water Quality 
Criteria for Outdoor bathing.  
 
A comparative assessment of the PRS identified in present study with previous assessment 
of PRS in 2018 reveals that total number of PRS have decreased in number from 351 (in 
2018) to 296 (in 2025). Significant reduction in number of PRS are observed in Priority I, IV 
and V.  
 
Detailed assessment indicates that improvement in water quality has been observed in 220 
polluted river stretches identified during year 2018. Out of 219 PRS, 149 river stretches are 
not identified as polluted stretches due to compliance of BOD criteria i.e value less than 3.0 
mg/ L. These stretches are located in Andhra Pradesh (03), Assam (41), Bihar (01), Goa 
(10), Gujarat (10), Himachal Pradesh (02), Jammu & Kashmir (03), Jharkhand (02), 
Karnataka (04), Kerala (09), Madhya Pradesh (11), Meghalaya (05), Mizoram (06), 
Nagaland (04), Odisha (14), Puducherry (01), Punjab (02), Sikkim (02), Telangana (04), 
Tripura (05), Uttarakhand (01) and West Bengal (09). Due to improvement in water quality, 
priority class of 71 previously identified polluted river stretches has been shifted to lower 
priority class. These stretches are in the States/ UTs of Andhra Pradesh (02), Assam (01), 
Daman, Diu & Dadra Nagar Haveli (01), Gujarat (02), Jammu and Kashmir (02), Jharkhand 
(01), Karnataka (05), Kerala (04), Madhya Pradesh (03), Maharashtra (29), Meghalaya (01), 
Mizoram (01), Nagaland (01), Odisha (03), Rajasthan (01), Tamil Nadu (02), Telangana 
(03), Uttar Pradesh (02), Uttarakhand (02) and West Bengal (04).  
 
The decrease in number of identified polluted river stretches which have shown 
improvement in the water quality could be attributed to the efforts being made by the 
respective State Government for development for infrastructure for sewage management, 
industrial effluent management, waste management and enforcement of regulations for 
prevention and control of pollution in rivers. 
 
State Pollution Control Boards (SPCBs) / Pollution Control Committees (PCCs) have 
provided inputs for preparation of the updated version of report.

CHAPTER 1 
INTRODUCTION 
 
The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974 has been enacted with the basic 
objectives of prevention and control of water pollution and maintaining and restoring 
wholesomeness of water in India. The main and important functions of the Central Pollution 
Control Board under the said Act are – to promote cleanliness of streams and wells in 
different areas of the States and to plan and cause to be executed a nation-wide program 
for the prevention, control or abatement of water pollution. The main and important function 
of the State Pollution Control Boards in this regard under the said Act is - to plan a 
comprehensive program for the prevention, control or abatement of pollution of streams and 
wells in the State and to secure the execution thereof. Other important functions of the 
Central Pollution Control Board under the said Act are - to co-ordinate the activities of the 
State Boards and to provide technical assistance and guidance to the State Boards.  
 
The water quality management is carried out in the country under the above provisions of 
The Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974. In order to help SPCBs to prepare 
action plans for the prevention, control or abatement of water pollution in States, CPCB is 
coordinating a nation-wide network for monitoring water quality of aquatic resources in the 
country. The monitoring of water quality was initiated during 1977-78 under Global 
Environmental Monitoring System (GEMS). The monitoring network has been gradually 
increased to cover the aquatic resources in the country viz. Rivers, Lakes, Tanks, Ponds, 
Drains, Water Treatment Plant, Sewage Treatment Plants, Coastal Waters, Wetlands and 
Ground water under the National Water Quality Monitoring Programme (NWMP).  
 
Presently monitoring of water quality of rivers under NWMP is carried out at 2155 locations 
on 645 rivers. The present NWMP monitoring network comprises of 4736 locations in 28 
States and 7 Union Territories spread over the country (no network in Andaman and Nicobar 
Islands). The monitoring network covers 2155 locations on Rivers, 909 on stagnant water 
bodies (Lakes, Ponds and Tanks), 227 on creeks/marine/sea/ coastal, 1233 on Wells and 
212 on other water bodies (drains, canals, WTPs/STPs).  
 
The water quality data indicates that rivers/ river water may not be polluted or contaminated 
throughout the length but only at few locations may be polluted where the self- assimilative 
 
Page 2
capacity of rivers is unable to maintain the quality. These locations/stretches on rivers are 
impacted with pollution which is required to be controlled. 
 
An exercise on identification of polluted river stretches was initiated during 2009 considering 
Biochemical Oxygen Demand (BOD) parameter, a criteria pollutant by assessment of water 
quality data for the period 2002–08 wherein 150 polluted river stretches were identified on 
121 rivers. A similar exercise was carried out by CPCB during 2015 for the data period 
2009–12 and 302 polluted river stretches were identified on 275 rivers.  
 
Subsequently, during September 2018, CPCB identified 351 Polluted river stretches in the 
country on 323 rivers considering water quality data of two years - the year 2016 and 2017. 
In compliance of the directions of Hon’ble NGT, action plans were prepared by State 
Governments and UT Administrations for rejuvenation of these 351 Polluted river stretches 
identified by CPCB. Concerned State/ UT Government departments are implementing the 
action plans and the progress is being reviewed periodically by the River Rejuvenation 
Committees (RRCs) at State Level and Central Monitoring Committee (CMC) constituted 
under the Chairmanship of Secretary, Ministry of Jal Shakti at Central Level. 
 
The last/latest exercise for identification of polluted river stretches was carried out during 
2022 wherein 311 polluted river stretches were identified in the country on 279 rivers based 
on assessment of water quality data of two years - the years 2019 & 2021 (2020 being 
pandemic year). 
 
OBJECTIVE OF REPORT 
 
Periodic analysis / assessment of water quality monitoring data of rivers is a pre-requisite to 
streamline the efforts and proper utilization of the resource for restoration of river water 
quality. In view of this, CPCB has been identifying polluted river stretches (PRS) in the 
country since the year 2009 as described above. The current exercise on identification of 
polluted river stretches in the country is an attempt to further corroborate the task of state 
governments of reviewing / updating the action plans for rejuvenation of polluted river 
stretches and the supervision and monitoring of their implementation by River Rejuvenation 
Committee (RRC) in the States/ UTs. 
 
 
Page 3
METHODOLOGY AND APPROACH 
 
River water quality monitoring at identified locations is carried out by collecting and analysing 
samples for field observations, physico-chemical, bacteriological parameters on monthly 
basis and for metals and pesticide twice a year, as per The Guidelines for Water Quality 
Monitoring, 2017 issued by Ministry of Environment, Forest & Climate Change (MoEF&CC). 
The water quality data so generated is submitted by SPCBs/ PCCs through online data entry 
portal namely Environmental Water Quality Data Entry System - EWQDES and stored 
centrally at CPCB server. 
 
CRITERIA FOR IDENTIFICATION AND CLASSIFICATION OF POLLUTED 
RIVER LOCATION/ STRETCH 
 
For identification of polluted river stretches, the water quality monitoring data for river is 
reviewed. The monitoring locations or river stretches which are non-compliant with the Bio-
chemical Oxygen Demand (BOD) limit in the Primary Water Quality Criteria for Bathing 
Water (used for organised outdoor bathing), i.e. BOD > 3.0 mg/L, are identified as polluted 
locations or polluted stretches.  
 
If there is a single location on river, rivulet or stream and the location is not complying to 
BOD, it is identified as Polluted River Location (PRL). Two or more polluted locations 
identified on a river in a continuous sequence are considered as a stretch and identified as 
Polluted River Stretch (PRS). 
 
PRL / PRS are categorized under five Priority Classes (I to V) on the basis of maximum 
BOD level observed. The criteria for prioritisation of river stretches is given below: 
 
Priority Class 
I 
: Monitoring locations exceeding BOD concentration 30.1 mg/ L 
Priority Class 
II 
: Monitoring locations having BOD between 20.1 – 30.0 mg/ L 
Priority Class III : Monitoring locations having BOD between 10.1 – 20.0 mg/ L 
Priority Class IV : Monitoring locations having BOD between 6.1 – 10.0 mg/ L 
Priority Class 
V : Monitoring locations having BOD between 3.1 – 6.0 mg/ L 
 
 
Page 4
CHAPTER 2 
 
IDENTIFICATION OF POLLUTED RIVER LOCATIONS/ STRETCHES  
 
The water quality data for years 2022 and 2023 is analysed and the monitoring locations 
exceeding the Primary Water Quality Criteria for Bathing Water (used for organised outdoor 
bathing), i.e. BOD > 3.0 mg/L, are identified as polluted river locations.  
Upon reviewing water quality data of 2116 locations monitored during the year 2022 and 
2023, it is observed that 804 river locations were exceeding the Primary Water Quality 
Criteria for Outdoor Bathing for the parameter BOD i.e. more than 3 mg/L. Based on the 
assessment, 296 polluted river stretches/locations have been identified on 271 rivers in 32 
States and Union Territories. List of 271 rivers having polluted river stretches is provided in 
Annexure-I and State-wise details of polluted stretches/locations is provided in Annexure-
II. Break up of these polluted river stretches in different priority classes is given in Table – 1

Of the 147 locations, 30 no. of locations are on rivers where there is one monitoring location 
on the river under NWMP which infers that in the upstream of the monitoring location there 
are possible sources of pollution which are to be identified for appropriate remedial action. 
These 30 polluted locations are on river - Maha, Chakpi, Irang, Thongjaorok, Sano, Jawai, 
Newta, Thirumanimutharu, Vasista, Sarabanga, Senki, Namchik, Dibang, Amravati 
(tributary), Kalpathipuzha, Garga, Bald, Ratta, Jaldhaka, Galudih, Kharkai, Koina, Piplad, 
Pamohi, Killiyar, Varattaar, Kothamangalam, Kannadipuzha, Leimakhong and Tupul. 
 
2.1 PRS IDENTIFIED UNDER PRIORITY CLASS – I  
 
37 Polluted river stretches have been identified in Priority Class – I (Refer Annexure – IV) 
in 14 States/ UTs with highest number i.e. 05 in Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh & Uttarakhand; 
04 in Gujarat; 03 in Karnataka; 02 in Haryana, Himachal Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, 
Maharashtra, Meghalaya & Punjab and 01 each in Assam, Delhi and West Bengal.  
 
2.2 PRS IDENTIFIED UNDER PRIORITY CLASS – II  
 
22 Polluted river stretches have been identified in Priority Class – II (Refer Annexure – V) 
in 13 States/ UTs with highest number i.e. 05 in Maharashtra; 03 in Himachal Pradesh; 02 
in Gujarat, Mizoram and Sikkim, and 01 each in Chhattisgarh, Daman- Diu, Goa, Haryana, 
Madhya Pradesh, Tamil Nadu, Telangana and Uttar Pradesh. 
 
2.3 PRS IDENTIFIED UNDER PRIORITY CLASS – III  
 
43 Polluted river stretches have been identified in Priority Class – III (Refer Annexure – VI) 
in 17 States/ UTs with highest number i.e. 09 in Maharashtra; 04 in Bihar, Karnataka and 
Uttarakhand; 03 each in Chhattisgarh and Telangana; 02 in Himachal Pradesh, Kerala, 
Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh and West Bengal; 01 each in Andhra Pradesh, Haryana, 
Jammu & Kashmir, Jharkhand, Mizoram and Odisha. 
 
2.4 PRS IDENTIFIED UNDER PRIORITY CLASS – IV  
 
 
Page 8
45 Polluted river stretches have been identified in Priority Class – IV (Refer Annexure – VII) 
in 17 States/ UTs with highest number i.e. 15 in Maharashtra; 04 in Kerala; 03 in Madhya 
Pradesh and Uttar Pradesh; 02 each in Bihar, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Jammu & 
Kashmir, Karnataka, Uttarakhand and West Bengal and 01 each in Assam, Jharkhand, 
Nagaland, Puducherry, Sikkim and Tamil Nadu.  
 
2.5 PRS IDENTIFIED UNDER PRIORITY CLASS – V  
 
149 Polluted river stretches have been identified in Priority Class – V (Refer Annexure – 
VIII) in 26 States/ UTs with highest number i.e. 26 in Kerala; 23 in Maharashtra; 18 in 
Manipur, 10 in Madhya Pradesh; 08 in Rajasthan & Jharkhand; 06 in Bihar and West Bengal; 
05 in Karnataka, Uttar Pradesh and Odisha; 04 in Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, Jammu & 
Kashmir; 03 in Andhra Pradesh; 02 in Chhattisgarh, Tamil Nadu and Gujarat and 01 each 
in Goa, Himachal Pradesh, Mizoram, Nagaland, Puducherry, Telangana, Tripura and 
Uttarakhand.

3.1 WATER QUALITY OF RIVERS IN ANDHRA PRADESH  
Water Quality of 17 rivers (Refer Annexure II) in Andhra Pradesh was monitored at 45 
locations during the year 2022 and 2023, out of which, 08 locations on 04 rivers were found 
non-complying to the prescribed Water Quality Criteria with respect to BOD. The names of 
04 polluted rivers are Gostani, Pennar, Kundu and Tungabhadra. Details of polluted river 
stretches identified are given in table below.

CHAPTER 4 
COMPARATIVE ASSESSMENT (2018 Vs 2025) 
All State Governments and UT Administrations have formed River Rejuvenation Committees 
(RRCs) of the concerned State/ UT; the RRC functions under the overall supervision and 
coordination of the Principal Secretary, Environment. The RRCs were responsible for 
developing action plans including timelines for implementation for the rejuvenation of the 
351 PRS identified by CPCB during the year 2018 for their respective States/ UTs. The 
action plans were prepared for bringing the water quality of polluted river stretches identified 
in 2018 to the quality level fit for bathing purposes (i.e. BOD ˂ 3 mg/L). Progress of 
implementation of action plans is reviewed periodically at state and by RRC and at Central 
level by CMC.  
A comparative assessment has been carried out as 2018 vs 2025 to assess the impact of 
implementation of action plans initiated for the purpose of rejuvenation of Polluted River 
Stretches identified in the year 2018, detailed in Table 4.1. The comparative assessment of 
polluted river stretches identified during 2018, 2022 and in the present study i.e. 2025 reveal 
that there is a decrease in number of identified polluted river stretches/ locations - 351 in the 
year 2018; 311 in the year 2022 and 296 in the year 2025.  
Table – 4.1: Comparative assessment of polluted river stretches

State wise comparative assessment of polluted river stretches identified in 2018, 2022 and

During the Year 2018, 351 polluted river stretches were identified on 323 rivers in the country 
based on analysis of water quality data of 521 rivers for the years 2016 and 2017. In present 
assessment, 296 polluted stretches are identified on 271 Rivers based on analysis of water 
quality data of 623 rivers for the years 2022 and 2023.

Based on the comparative assessment, following observations are made. 
 Maharashtra has the highest number of polluted river stretches in the country i.e. 54. In 
Year 2018, 53 polluted river stretches were identified. 
 Significant decrease in number of identified polluted river stretches has been observed in 
the State of Assam from 44 in year 2018 to 06 in 2025. Many of the rivers identified during 
the year 2018 were declared as wetlands and stagnant water bodies by Assam 
Government, which are not considered in this exercise. 
 In 15 States viz. Andhra Pradesh, Goa, Gujarat, Jammu & Kashmir, Karnataka, Madhya 
Pradesh, Meghalaya, Mizoram, Nagaland, Odisha, Punjab, Sikkim, Telangana, Tripura 
and West Bengal, number of identified polluted river stretches have reduced. 
 Number of polluted river stretches have increased in 12 States/UTs viz. Bihar, 
Chhattisgarh, Haryana (due to bifurcation of PRS on river Yamuna), Himachal Pradesh, 
Jharkhand, Kerala, Maharashtra, Manipur, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh and 
Uttarakhand.  
 No change has been observed in number of identified polluted river stretches in 03 
States/UTs viz. Daman and Diu, Dadra and Nagar Haveli (1), Delhi (1) and Puducherry 
(1). 
 PRS identified in Arunachal Pradesh due to availability of water quality data of rivers as 
previously these sanctioned locations were not monitored by the State.

4.5 CONCLUDING REMARKS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
 Over the years, monitoring network on rivers under NWMP has been enhanced from 
1533 river monitoring locations in year 2017 to 2155 river monitoring locations in year 
2023.  
 Decline in number of polluted river stretches are observed mainly due to collective 
actions taken by various stakeholders for rejuvenation of the polluted river stretches 
identified during the year 2018.  
 Number of polluted river stretches are reduced from 45 to 37 in Priority I, from 72 to 
44 in Priority IV and 175 to 149 in Priority V category.  
 An increasing trend is observed in number of identified PRS under Priority II (16 to 
22) which is attributed to shifting of few Priority I stretches to lower Priority Class, 
which is indicative of efforts initiated by State Govt. Agencies for rejuvenation of the 
PRS and no change in Priority III (43 - PRS) is observed.  
Water quality status of the 807 locations covered under the present PRS assessment during 
the year 2024 w.r.t. BOD is also provided in Annexure – XIV.

CHAPTER 5 
ACTIONS FOR REJUVENATION OF POLLUTED RIVER STRETCHES 
 
The water quality assessment of aquatic resources on long term basis provided information on 
the segments of rivers that are not meeting water quality criteria and have been identified as 
polluted. The database presented in the report acts as background information for formulation of 
action plan/ plans by the concerned State/UT Government for restoration of polluted river 
stretches,  
 
The action plan for restoration may include source control like identification and management of 
polluting sources including functioning/ status of STPs/ETPs/CETP and solid waste management 
and processing facilities, quantification and characterization of solid waste, trade and sewage 
generated in the catchment area of polluted river stretch.  
 
Action plan may also include river catchment/basin management and flood plain zone protection 
and its management such as adopting good irrigation practices, protection and management of 
Flood Plain Zones (FPZ), rain water harvesting, ground water charging, maintaining minimum 
environmental flow of river and plantation on both sides of the river. Setting up of biodiversity parks 
on flood plains by removing encroachment shall also be considered as an important component 
for river rejuvenation.  
 
Action plan should focus on proper interception and diversion of sewage carrying drains to the 
Sewage Treatment Plant (STP) and emphasis should be on utilization of treated sewage so as to 
minimize extraction of ground or surface water. Also, the action plan should have definite or 
specific timelines for execution of steps.  
 
 
Collective efforts of various concerned agencies of State/UT Government with effective public 
participation is required for effective Water Quality Management.
